Sometimes, yes… I think so. It’s not like a whole conversation, exactly. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear a woman sort of mutter my name from the polishing room, and I’ll go look, because it feels like someone is back there.

Like if someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, I start with the repair and then the invoice because that’s part of the job, and then I’ll think about paperwork, and then suddenly I’m talking about mortgage papers I need to find at home, or even garden soil because I was supposed to pick some up, and… I know that sounds unrelated.\n\nIn my head it’s like there’s a little bridge between each thing, but by the time I say it out loud, other people don’t see the bridge. I can tell from their faces that I’ve lost them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a strong sense that people are trying to harm you or are talking about you?\n\nPatient: I mean… harm, I don’t know if I’d say harm exactly. But talking about me, or checking up on me, yes, sometimes it feels that way.\n\nLike, some customers come in and they ask all these questions about the new showroom—when we did it, how much work it was, whether business is good—and I know my husband says they’re just being friendly, making conversation. But I get this strong feeling they’re really trying to see if I’m going to miss payments or if we’re in trouble somehow. And then I start thinking about the loan papers and the display cases and how the lighting guy never sent the right receipt… sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, I do feel like some people are watching for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… I think so. The polishing room has that sound to it anyway, even when the machines are off, so I tell myself it’s probably just… the building or my ears or something. And then I start thinking about the loan papers and the display cases and how the lighting guy never sent the right receipt… sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, I do feel like some people are watching for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… I think so. It’s not like a whole conversation, exactly. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear a woman sort of mutter my name from the polishing room, and I’ll go look, because it feels like someone is back there.\n\nAnd then sometimes I smell burning metal when the torches are off, which—well, that isn’t a voice, I know, but in a jewelry shop that makes you check everything twice. The polishing room has that sound to it anyway, even when the machines are off, so I tell myself it’s probably just… the building or my ears or something. But it feels real enough that I go check.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most mornings, yes… I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about opening the cases and whether the lights will come on evenly, because if one bulb is out it makes everything look dull, and then I wonder if maybe it’s just me seeing it that way. But… yes, it’s there a lot of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were hearing the woman call your name or felt people were checking up on you, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was a period like that. Before the depression really settled in, I was still doing a lot—I was running classes at the shop, and I was out in the garden after work, fussing with the tomatoes and the soil, you know, trying to get the beds right.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I’d hear things from the polishing room when no one was there, or I’d get that feeling that customers were sort of… checking on the business, watching to see how we were doing. And I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness then, not really. It came more strongly later.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing your name or feeling people were checking up on you, and you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it was at least a few weeks. It was before the depression really took hold. I was still running the little classes at the shop, showing people how to set stones and all that, and I’d go home and mess around in the garden—tomatoes, mostly, though they never come in evenly.\n\nBut even then, when I wasn’t really depressed, I kept hearing something from the polishing room, like my name or a noise that made me check. And I had this worry that customers were watching the business too closely, like checking up on us. So… yes, that did happen when I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness yet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But… yes, it’s there a lot of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were hearing the woman call your name or felt people were checking up on you, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was a period like that. Before the depression really settled in, I was still doing a lot—I was running classes at the shop, and I was out in the garden after work, fussing with the tomatoes and the soil, you know, trying to get the beds right.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I’d hear things from the polishing room when no one was there, or I’d get that feeling that customers were sort of… checking on the business, watching to see how we were doing. And I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness then, not really. It came more strongly later.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing your name or feeling people were checking up on you, and you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it was at least a few weeks. It was before the depression really took hold. I was still running the little classes at the shop, showing people how to set stones and all that, and I’d go home and mess around in the garden—tomatoes, mostly, though they never come in evenly.\n\nBut even then, when I wasn’t really depressed, I kept hearing something from the polishing room, like my name or a noise that made me check. So… yes, that did happen when I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness yet.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after any major medical illness, head injury, or seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I can point to. No big accident or seizure that I know of.\n\nI did once bump my head on the edge of a display case years ago—those glass corners are awful, and I told my assistant we needed better padding—but it wasn’t anything major. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming less expressive emotionally, like your face showing less feeling or your voice sounding flatter than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed my face doing anything different, exactly. My sister once said I looked “tired,” but she says that when I don’t wear lipstick too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nMy voice… I don’t think it’s flatter. At the shop I still talk to people about stones and settings, and sometimes I probably sound too interested, honestly, if someone asks about opals. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that, but maybe other people would know better than me.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems with hearing your name, feeling people were checking up on you, and your speech getting hard to follow been going on for 6 months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… longer than six months. It’s been, I guess, almost two years now, since not long after we took on the business mortgage.\n\nThat whole period is a little tangled in my head, because the mortgage paperwork was such a mess, and I remember thinking the bank forms were printed in the tiniest font… but yes, the hearing my name and feeling like people were checking up on me, and the talking getting hard to follow sometimes—those changes have been going on since around then. Yes… people have said that. Mostly customers, and my assistant too, in a nicer way.

Like if someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, I start with the repair and then the invoice because that’s part of the job, and then I’ll think about paperwork, and then suddenly I’m talking about mortgage papers I need to find at home, or even garden soil because I was supposed to pick some up, and… I know that sounds unrelated.\n\nIn my head it’s like there’s a little bridge between each thing, but by the time I say it out loud, other people don’t see the bridge. I can tell from their faces that I’ve lost them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a strong sense that people are trying to harm you or are talking about you?\n\nPatient: I mean… harm, I don’t know if I’d say harm exactly. But talking about me, or checking up on me, yes, sometimes it feels that way.\n\nLike, some customers come in and they ask all these questions about the new showroom—when we did it, how much work it was, whether business is good—and I know my husband says they’re just being friendly, making conversation. But I get this strong feeling they’re really trying to see if I’m going to miss payments or if we’re in trouble somehow. And then I start thinking about the loan papers and the display cases and how the lighting guy never sent the right receipt… sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, I do feel like some people are watching for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… I think so. It’s not like a whole conversation, exactly. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear a woman sort of mutter my name from the polishing room, and I’ll go look, because it feels like someone is back there.\n\nAnd then sometimes I smell burning metal when the torches are off, which—well, that isn’t a voice, I know, but in a jewelry shop that makes you check everything twice. The polishing room has that sound to it anyway, even when the machines are off, so I tell myself it’s probably just… the building or my ears or something. But it feels real enough that I go check.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most mornings, yes… I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about opening the cases and whether the lights will come on evenly, because if one bulb is out it makes everything look dull, and then I wonder if maybe it’s just me seeing it that way. But… yes, it’s there a lot of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were hearing the woman call your name or felt people were checking up on you, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was a period like that. Before the depression really settled in, I was still doing a lot—I was running classes at the shop, and I was out in the garden after work, fussing with the tomatoes and the soil, you know, trying to get the beds right.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I’d hear things from the polishing room when no one was there, or I’d get that feeling that customers were sort of… checking on the business, watching to see how we were doing. And I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness then, not really. It came more strongly later.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing your name or feeling people were checking up on you, and you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it was at least a few weeks. It was before the depression really took hold. I was still running the little classes at the shop, showing people how to set stones and all that, and I’d go home and mess around in the garden—tomatoes, mostly, though they never come in evenly.\n\nBut even then, when I wasn’t really depressed, I kept hearing something from the polishing room, like my name or a noise that made me check. And I had this worry that customers were watching the business too closely, like checking up on us. So… yes, that did happen when I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness yet.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after any major medical illness, head injury, or seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I can point to. No big accident or seizure that I know of.\n\nI did once bump my head on the edge of a display case years ago—those glass corners are awful, and I told my assistant we needed better padding—but it wasn’t anything major. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming less expressive emotionally, like your face showing less feeling or your voice sounding flatter than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed my face doing anything different, exactly. My sister once said I looked “tired,” but she says that when I don’t wear lipstick too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nMy voice… I don’t think it’s flatter. At the shop I still talk to people about stones and settings, and sometimes I probably sound too interested, honestly, if someone asks about opals. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that, but maybe other people would know better than me.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems with hearing your name, feeling people were checking up on you, and your speech getting hard to follow been going on for 6 months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… longer than six months. It’s been, I guess, almost two years now, since not long after we took on the business mortgage.\n\nThat whole period is a little tangled in my head, because the mortgage paperwork was such a mess, and I remember thinking the bank forms were printed in the tiniest font… but yes, the hearing my name and feeling like people were checking up on me, and the talking getting hard to follow sometimes—those changes have been going on since around then. Almost two years.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is often hard to follow or doesn't make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes… people have said that. Mostly customers, and my assistant too, in a nicer way.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, actually. Someone will ask me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil—because I had to repot the basil by the back window—and then the mortgage papers from the business, and I can see their face change like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away, but I can tell afterward that I lost the point somewhere.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Mostly customers, and my assistant too, in a nicer way.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, actually. Someone will ask me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil—because I had to repot the basil by the back window—and then the mortgage papers from the business, and I can see their face change like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away, but I can tell afterward that I lost the point somewhere.\n\nDoctor: When your speech becomes hard for people to follow, is that usually happening even when you are not hearing your name or feeling that people are checking up on you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it can happen even when I’m not hearing anything like that or feeling checked on. That’s actually why I came in, because it’ll be something ordinary—someone asks when a ring repair will be ready—and I mean to answer plainly.\n\nBut then I’m talking about the invoice, and then I’ll jump to the garden soil, because I was thinking about repotting something by the window, and then somehow I’m back on the mortgage papers… and I can see their eyes sort of go blank, like I’ve taken them somewhere they didn’t ask to go. So yes, it’s not only when those other things are happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But yes, I do feel like some people are watching for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… I think so. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear a woman sort of mutter my name from the polishing room, and I’ll go look, because it feels like someone is back there.\n\nAnd then sometimes I smell burning metal when the torches are off, which—well, that isn’t a voice, I know, but in a jewelry shop that makes you check everything twice. The polishing room has that sound to it anyway, even when the machines are off, so I tell myself it’s probably just… the building or my ears or something. But it feels real enough that I go check.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most mornings, yes… I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about opening the cases and whether the lights will come on evenly, because if one bulb is out it makes everything look dull, and then I wonder if maybe it’s just me seeing it that way. But… yes, it’s there a lot of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were hearing the woman call your name or felt people were checking up on you, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was a period like that. Before the depression really settled in, I was still doing a lot—I was running classes at the shop, and I was out in the garden after work, fussing with the tomatoes and the soil, you know, trying to get the beds right.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I’d hear things from the polishing room when no one was there, or I’d get that feeling that customers were sort of… checking on the business, watching to see how we were doing. And I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness then, not really. It came more strongly later.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing your name or feeling people were checking up on you, and you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it was at least a few weeks. It was before the depression really took hold. I was still running the little classes at the shop, showing people how to set stones and all that, and I’d go home and mess around in the garden—tomatoes, mostly, though they never come in evenly.\n\nBut even then, when I wasn’t really depressed, I kept hearing something from the polishing room, like my name or a noise that made me check. And I had this worry that customers were watching the business too closely, like checking up on us. So… yes, that did happen when I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness yet.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after any major medical illness, head injury, or seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I can point to. No big accident or seizure that I know of.\n\nI did once bump my head on the edge of a display case years ago—those glass corners are awful, and I told my assistant we needed better padding—but it wasn’t anything major. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming less expressive emotionally, like your face showing less feeling or your voice sounding flatter than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed my face doing anything different, exactly. My sister once said I looked “tired,” but she says that when I don’t wear lipstick too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nMy voice… I don’t think it’s flatter. At the shop I still talk to people about stones and settings, and sometimes I probably sound too interested, honestly, if someone asks about opals. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that, but maybe other people would know better than me.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems with hearing your name, feeling people were checking up on you, and your speech getting hard to follow been going on for 6 months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… longer than six months. It’s been, I guess, almost two years now, since not long after we took on the business mortgage.\n\nThat whole period is a little tangled in my head, because the mortgage paperwork was such a mess, and I remember thinking the bank forms were printed in the tiniest font… but yes, the hearing my name and feeling like people were checking up on me, and the talking getting hard to follow sometimes—those changes have been going on since around then. Almost two years.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is often hard to follow or doesn't make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes… people have said that. Mostly customers, and my assistant too, in a nicer way.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, actually. Someone will ask me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil—because I had to repot the basil by the back window—and then the mortgage papers from the business, and I can see their face change like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away, but I can tell afterward that I lost the point somewhere.\n\nDoctor: When your speech becomes hard for people to follow, is that usually happening even when you are not hearing your name or feeling that people are checking up on you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it can happen even when I’m not hearing anything like that or feeling checked on. That’s actually why I came in, because it’ll be something ordinary—someone asks when a ring repair will be ready—and I mean to answer plainly.\n\nBut then I’m talking about the invoice, and then I’ll jump to the garden soil, because I was thinking about repotting something by the window, and then somehow I’m back on the mortgage papers… and I can see their eyes sort of go blank, like I’ve taken them somewhere they didn’t ask to go. So yes, it’s not only when those other things are happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been behaving in ways that other people find very disorganized or odd?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “odd” is hard for me to judge because people think jewelry people are odd anyway. I’ll get very particular about a setting or a stone tray being just so, and then someone says I’m acting strange, but I think I’m just trying not to lose a tiny sapphire, you know?\n\nI don’t think I’ve been doing anything very wild or… disorganized like that, not that I can point to. Maybe I’d have to ask what you mean exactly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates":["F25.1","F20.0","F20.1"],"note":"","is_final":false}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think anyone has said it that plainly, like “bizarre.” My assistant has said I seem scattered sometimes, but she says that when I’ve moved three trays and then can’t remember which one has the clasp I was working on… and honestly that happens in a jewelry shop, things are small, the light catches them—

Sorry. I don’t know. I haven’t had people tell me my behavior is very disorganized or bizarre, not in those words. Maybe they notice things I don’t, but I can’t point to anything like that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.1, F20.0, F20.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess… I came in because talking has been getting strange for me. Like at the shop, someone asks me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I start answering but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, and then mortgage papers I need to find, and I can see their face just sort of… drift. Like they’re losing me, or I’m losing them. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually high or unusually energetic lately?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually high? I don’t think so. Energetic, not really that I’ve noticed. I mean, I’ll get going on something at the bench sometimes—like sorting little findings or checking stones—and then I realize I’ve mixed up what tray I was even using, but that’s not the same as feeling high, is it?\n\nI’m not sure I totally know what counts as “unusually.” I don’t feel like I’m bouncing off the walls or anything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking and your thoughts jump from one thing to another, does it happen because the ideas feel connected in your mind?\n\nPatient: I think they do feel connected, at least at the time. Like if someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, I start with the repair and then the invoice because that’s part of the job, and then I’ll think about paperwork, and then suddenly I’m talking about mortgage papers I need to find at home, or even garden soil because I was supposed to pick some up, and… I know that sounds unrelated.\n\nIn my head it’s like there’s a little bridge between each thing, but by the time I say it out loud, other people don’t see the bridge. I can tell from their faces that I’ve lost them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a strong sense that people are trying to harm you or are talking about you?\n\nPatient: I mean… harm, I don’t know if I’d say harm exactly. But talking about me, or checking up on me, yes, sometimes it feels that way.\n\nLike, some customers come in and they ask all these questions about the new showroom—when we did it, how much work it was, whether business is good—and I know my husband says they’re just being friendly, making conversation. But I get this strong feeling they’re really trying to see if I’m going to miss payments or if we’re in trouble somehow. And then I start thinking about the loan papers and the display cases and how the lighting guy never sent the right receipt… sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, I do feel like some people are watching for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… I think so. It’s not like a whole conversation, exactly. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear a woman sort of mutter my name from the polishing room, and I’ll go look, because it feels like someone is back there.\n\nAnd then sometimes I smell burning metal when the torches are off, which—well, that isn’t a voice, I know, but in a jewelry shop that makes you check everything twice. The polishing room has that sound to it anyway, even when the machines are off, so I tell myself it’s probably just… the building or my ears or something. But it feels real enough that I go check.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most mornings, yes… I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about opening the cases and whether the lights will come on evenly, because if one bulb is out it makes everything look dull, and then I wonder if maybe it’s just me seeing it that way. But… yes, it’s there a lot of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were hearing the woman call your name or felt people were checking up on you, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was a period like that. Before the depression really settled in, I was still doing a lot—I was running classes at the shop, and I was out in the garden after work, fussing with the tomatoes and the soil, you know, trying to get the beds right.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I’d hear things from the polishing room when no one was there, or I’d get that feeling that customers were sort of… checking on the business, watching to see how we were doing. And I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness then, not really. It came more strongly later.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing your name or feeling people were checking up on you, and you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it was at least a few weeks. It was before the depression really took hold. I was still running the little classes at the shop, showing people how to set stones and all that, and I’d go home and mess around in the garden—tomatoes, mostly, though they never come in evenly.\n\nBut even then, when I wasn’t really depressed, I kept hearing something from the polishing room, like my name or a noise that made me check. And I had this worry that customers were watching the business too closely, like checking up on us. So… yes, that did happen when I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness yet.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after any major medical illness, head injury, or seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I can point to. No big accident or seizure that I know of.\n\nI did once bump my head on the edge of a display case years ago—those glass corners are awful, and I told my assistant we needed better padding—but it wasn’t anything major. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming less expressive emotionally, like your face showing less feeling or your voice sounding flatter than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed my face doing anything different, exactly. My sister once said I looked “tired,” but she says that when I don’t wear lipstick too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nMy voice… I don’t think it’s flatter. At the shop I still talk to people about stones and settings, and sometimes I probably sound too interested, honestly, if someone asks about opals. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that, but maybe other people would know better than me.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems with hearing your name, feeling people were checking up on you, and your speech getting hard to follow been going on for 6 months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… longer than six months. It’s been, I guess, almost two years now, since not long after we took on the business mortgage.\n\nThat whole period is a little tangled in my head, because the mortgage paperwork was such a mess, and I remember thinking the bank forms were printed in the tiniest font… but yes, the hearing my name and feeling like people were checking up on me, and the talking getting hard to follow sometimes—those changes have been going on since around then. Almost two years.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is often hard to follow or doesn't make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes… people have said that. Mostly customers, and my assistant too, in a nicer way.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, actually. Someone will ask me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil—because I had to repot the basil by the back window—and then the mortgage papers from the business, and I can see their face change like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away, but I can tell afterward that I lost the point somewhere.\n\nDoctor: When your speech becomes hard for people to follow, is that usually happening even when you are not hearing your name or feeling that people are checking up on you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it can happen even when I’m not hearing anything like that or feeling checked on. That’s actually why I came in, because it’ll be something ordinary—someone asks when a ring repair will be ready—and I mean to answer plainly.\n\nBut then I’m talking about the invoice, and then I’ll jump to the garden soil, because I was thinking about repotting something by the window, and then somehow I’m back on the mortgage papers… and I can see their eyes sort of go blank, like I’ve taken them somewhere they didn’t ask to go. So yes, it’s not only when those other things are happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been behaving in ways that other people find very disorganized or odd?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “odd” is hard for me to judge because people think jewelry people are odd anyway. I’ll get very particular about a setting or a stone tray being just so, and then someone says I’m acting strange, but I think I’m just trying not to lose a tiny sapphire, you know?\n\nI don’t think I’ve been doing anything very wild or… disorganized like that, not that I can point to. Maybe I’d have to ask what you mean exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your behavior seems very disorganized or bizarre?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has said it that plainly, like “bizarre.” My assistant has said I seem scattered sometimes, but she says that when I’ve moved three trays and then can’t remember which one has the clasp I was working on… and honestly that happens in a jewelry shop, things are small, the light catches them—\n\nSorry. I don’t know. I haven’t had people tell me my behavior is very disorganized or bizarre, not in those words. Maybe they notice things I don’t, but I can’t point to anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think so. I still want to do things, at least in my head. I used to get really excited hunting for old clasps, the unusual ones with the little hinge that still moves smoothly, or planning small jewelry classes—like showing people how to tell when a setting is too worn.

But lately I mostly just stand there and stare at the trays. I’ll think, “I should start sorting these,” or “I should sketch out that class,” and then it all feels… not worth starting. Like the first step is too far away. And then I end up just moving one tray from one side of the bench to the other, which isn’t really doing anything.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.1, F20.0, F20.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess… I came in because talking has been getting strange for me. Like at the shop, someone asks me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I start answering but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, and then mortgage papers I need to find, and I can see their face just sort of… drift. Like they’re losing me, or I’m losing them. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually high or unusually energetic lately?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually high? I don’t think so. Energetic, not really that I’ve noticed. I mean, I’ll get going on something at the bench sometimes—like sorting little findings or checking stones—and then I realize I’ve mixed up what tray I was even using, but that’s not the same as feeling high, is it?\n\nI’m not sure I totally know what counts as “unusually.” I don’t feel like I’m bouncing off the walls or anything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking and your thoughts jump from one thing to another, does it happen because the ideas feel connected in your mind?\n\nPatient: I think they do feel connected, at least at the time. Like if someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, I start with the repair and then the invoice because that’s part of the job, and then I’ll think about paperwork, and then suddenly I’m talking about mortgage papers I need to find at home, or even garden soil because I was supposed to pick some up, and… I know that sounds unrelated.\n\nIn my head it’s like there’s a little bridge between each thing, but by the time I say it out loud, other people don’t see the bridge. I can tell from their faces that I’ve lost them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a strong sense that people are trying to harm you or are talking about you?\n\nPatient: I mean… harm, I don’t know if I’d say harm exactly. But talking about me, or checking up on me, yes, sometimes it feels that way.\n\nLike, some customers come in and they ask all these questions about the new showroom—when we did it, how much work it was, whether business is good—and I know my husband says they’re just being friendly, making conversation. But I get this strong feeling they’re really trying to see if I’m going to miss payments or if we’re in trouble somehow. And then I start thinking about the loan papers and the display cases and how the lighting guy never sent the right receipt… sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, I do feel like some people are watching for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… I think so. It’s not like a whole conversation, exactly. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear a woman sort of mutter my name from the polishing room, and I’ll go look, because it feels like someone is back there.\n\nAnd then sometimes I smell burning metal when the torches are off, which—well, that isn’t a voice, I know, but in a jewelry shop that makes you check everything twice. The polishing room has that sound to it anyway, even when the machines are off, so I tell myself it’s probably just… the building or my ears or something. But it feels real enough that I go check.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most mornings, yes… I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about opening the cases and whether the lights will come on evenly, because if one bulb is out it makes everything look dull, and then I wonder if maybe it’s just me seeing it that way. But… yes, it’s there a lot of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were hearing the woman call your name or felt people were checking up on you, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was a period like that. Before the depression really settled in, I was still doing a lot—I was running classes at the shop, and I was out in the garden after work, fussing with the tomatoes and the soil, you know, trying to get the beds right.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I’d hear things from the polishing room when no one was there, or I’d get that feeling that customers were sort of… checking on the business, watching to see how we were doing. And I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness then, not really. It came more strongly later.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing your name or feeling people were checking up on you, and you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it was at least a few weeks. It was before the depression really took hold. I was still running the little classes at the shop, showing people how to set stones and all that, and I’d go home and mess around in the garden—tomatoes, mostly, though they never come in evenly.\n\nBut even then, when I wasn’t really depressed, I kept hearing something from the polishing room, like my name or a noise that made me check. And I had this worry that customers were watching the business too closely, like checking up on us. So… yes, that did happen when I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness yet.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after any major medical illness, head injury, or seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I can point to. No big accident or seizure that I know of.\n\nI did once bump my head on the edge of a display case years ago—those glass corners are awful, and I told my assistant we needed better padding—but it wasn’t anything major. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming less expressive emotionally, like your face showing less feeling or your voice sounding flatter than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed my face doing anything different, exactly. My sister once said I looked “tired,” but she says that when I don’t wear lipstick too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nMy voice… I don’t think it’s flatter. At the shop I still talk to people about stones and settings, and sometimes I probably sound too interested, honestly, if someone asks about opals. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that, but maybe other people would know better than me.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems with hearing your name, feeling people were checking up on you, and your speech getting hard to follow been going on for 6 months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… longer than six months. It’s been, I guess, almost two years now, since not long after we took on the business mortgage.\n\nThat whole period is a little tangled in my head, because the mortgage paperwork was such a mess, and I remember thinking the bank forms were printed in the tiniest font… but yes, the hearing my name and feeling like people were checking up on me, and the talking getting hard to follow sometimes—those changes have been going on since around then. Almost two years.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is often hard to follow or doesn't make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes… people have said that. Mostly customers, and my assistant too, in a nicer way.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, actually. Someone will ask me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil—because I had to repot the basil by the back window—and then the mortgage papers from the business, and I can see their face change like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away, but I can tell afterward that I lost the point somewhere.\n\nDoctor: When your speech becomes hard for people to follow, is that usually happening even when you are not hearing your name or feeling that people are checking up on you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it can happen even when I’m not hearing anything like that or feeling checked on. So yes, it’s not only when those other things are happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been behaving in ways that other people find very disorganized or odd?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “odd” is hard for me to judge because people think jewelry people are odd anyway. Like at the shop, someone asks me something simple, like when their ring repair will be ready, and I start answering but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil, and then mortgage papers I need to find, and I can see their face just sort of… drift. Like they’re losing me, or I’m losing them. It’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually high or unusually energetic lately?\n\nPatient: Um… unusually high? I don’t think so. Energetic, not really that I’ve noticed. I mean, I’ll get going on something at the bench sometimes—like sorting little findings or checking stones—and then I realize I’ve mixed up what tray I was even using, but that’s not the same as feeling high, is it?\n\nI’m not sure I totally know what counts as “unusually.” I don’t feel like I’m bouncing off the walls or anything.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking and your thoughts jump from one thing to another, does it happen because the ideas feel connected in your mind?\n\nPatient: I think they do feel connected, at least at the time. Like if someone asks when a ring repair will be ready, I start with the repair and then the invoice because that’s part of the job, and then I’ll think about paperwork, and then suddenly I’m talking about mortgage papers I need to find at home, or even garden soil because I was supposed to pick some up, and… I know that sounds unrelated.\n\nIn my head it’s like there’s a little bridge between each thing, but by the time I say it out loud, other people don’t see the bridge. I can tell from their faces that I’ve lost them.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a strong sense that people are trying to harm you or are talking about you?\n\nPatient: I mean… harm, I don’t know if I’d say harm exactly. But talking about me, or checking up on me, yes, sometimes it feels that way.\n\nLike, some customers come in and they ask all these questions about the new showroom—when we did it, how much work it was, whether business is good—and I know my husband says they’re just being friendly, making conversation. But I get this strong feeling they’re really trying to see if I’m going to miss payments or if we’re in trouble somehow. And then I start thinking about the loan papers and the display cases and how the lighting guy never sent the right receipt… sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, I do feel like some people are watching for something.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one is there?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… I think so. It’s not like a whole conversation, exactly. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear a woman sort of mutter my name from the polishing room, and I’ll go look, because it feels like someone is back there.\n\nAnd then sometimes I smell burning metal when the torches are off, which—well, that isn’t a voice, I know, but in a jewelry shop that makes you check everything twice. The polishing room has that sound to it anyway, even when the machines are off, so I tell myself it’s probably just… the building or my ears or something. But it feels real enough that I go check.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most mornings, yes… I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about opening the cases and whether the lights will come on evenly, because if one bulb is out it makes everything look dull, and then I wonder if maybe it’s just me seeing it that way. But… yes, it’s there a lot of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were hearing the woman call your name or felt people were checking up on you, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was a period like that. Before the depression really settled in, I was still doing a lot—I was running classes at the shop, and I was out in the garden after work, fussing with the tomatoes and the soil, you know, trying to get the beds right.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I’d hear things from the polishing room when no one was there, or I’d get that feeling that customers were sort of… checking on the business, watching to see how we were doing. And I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness then, not really. It came more strongly later.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing your name or feeling people were checking up on you, and you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it was at least a few weeks. It was before the depression really took hold. I was still running the little classes at the shop, showing people how to set stones and all that, and I’d go home and mess around in the garden—tomatoes, mostly, though they never come in evenly.\n\nBut even then, when I wasn’t really depressed, I kept hearing something from the polishing room, like my name or a noise that made me check. And I had this worry that customers were watching the business too closely, like checking up on us. So… yes, that did happen when I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness yet.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after any major medical illness, head injury, or seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I can point to. No big accident or seizure that I know of.\n\nI did once bump my head on the edge of a display case years ago—those glass corners are awful, and I told my assistant we needed better padding—but it wasn’t anything major. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming less expressive emotionally, like your face showing less feeling or your voice sounding flatter than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed my face doing anything different, exactly. My sister once said I looked “tired,” but she says that when I don’t wear lipstick too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nMy voice… I don’t think it’s flatter. At the shop I still talk to people about stones and settings, and sometimes I probably sound too interested, honestly, if someone asks about opals. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that, but maybe other people would know better than me.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems with hearing your name, feeling people were checking up on you, and your speech getting hard to follow been going on for 6 months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… longer than six months. It’s been, I guess, almost two years now, since not long after we took on the business mortgage.\n\nThat whole period is a little tangled in my head, because the mortgage paperwork was such a mess, and I remember thinking the bank forms were printed in the tiniest font… but yes, the hearing my name and feeling like people were checking up on me, and the talking getting hard to follow sometimes—those changes have been going on since around then. Almost two years.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is often hard to follow or doesn't make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes… people have said that. Mostly customers, and my assistant too, in a nicer way.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, actually. Someone will ask me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil—because I had to repot the basil by the back window—and then the mortgage papers from the business, and I can see their face change like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away, but I can tell afterward that I lost the point somewhere.\n\nDoctor: When your speech becomes hard for people to follow, is that usually happening even when you are not hearing your name or feeling that people are checking up on you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it can happen even when I’m not hearing anything like that or feeling checked on. That’s actually why I came in, because it’ll be something ordinary—someone asks when a ring repair will be ready—and I mean to answer plainly.\n\nBut then I’m talking about the invoice, and then I’ll jump to the garden soil, because I was thinking about repotting something by the window, and then somehow I’m back on the mortgage papers… and I can see their eyes sort of go blank, like I’ve taken them somewhere they didn’t ask to go. So yes, it’s not only when those other things are happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been behaving in ways that other people find very disorganized or odd?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “odd” is hard for me to judge because people think jewelry people are odd anyway. I’ll get very particular about a setting or a stone tray being just so, and then someone says I’m acting strange, but I think I’m just trying not to lose a tiny sapphire, you know?\n\nI don’t think I’ve been doing anything very wild or… disorganized like that, not that I can point to. Maybe I’d have to ask what you mean exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your behavior seems very disorganized or bizarre?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has said it that plainly, like “bizarre.” My assistant has said I seem scattered sometimes, but she says that when I’ve moved three trays and then can’t remember which one has the clasp I was working on… and honestly that happens in a jewelry shop, things are small, the light catches them—\n\nSorry. I don’t know. I haven’t had people tell me my behavior is very disorganized or bizarre, not in those words. Maybe they notice things I don’t, but I can’t point to anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost motivation to do everyday things, like work, chores, or hobbies, even when you want to do them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. I used to get really excited hunting for old clasps, the unusual ones with the little hinge that still moves smoothly, or planning small jewelry classes—like showing people how to tell when a setting is too worn.\n\nBut lately I mostly just stand there and stare at the trays. When the shop is really quiet, I’ll hear a woman sort of mutter my name from the polishing room, and I’ll go look, because it feels like someone is back there.\n\nAnd then sometimes I smell burning metal when the torches are off, which—well, that isn’t a voice, I know, but in a jewelry shop that makes you check everything twice. The polishing room has that sound to it anyway, even when the machines are off, so I tell myself it’s probably just… the building or my ears or something. But it feels real enough that I go check.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most mornings, yes… I wake up with this flat, heavy sadness, like the color has drained out of the house before I even get to the shop.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about opening the cases and whether the lights will come on evenly, because if one bulb is out it makes everything look dull, and then I wonder if maybe it’s just me seeing it that way. But… yes, it’s there a lot of the day, most days.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were hearing the woman call your name or felt people were checking up on you, but you were not feeling depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think there was a period like that. Before the depression really settled in, I was still doing a lot—I was running classes at the shop, and I was out in the garden after work, fussing with the tomatoes and the soil, you know, trying to get the beds right.\n\nBut even then, for several weeks, I’d hear things from the polishing room when no one was there, or I’d get that feeling that customers were sort of… checking on the business, watching to see how we were doing. And I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness then, not really. It came more strongly later.\n\nDoctor: Was there ever a period of at least two weeks when you were hearing your name or feeling people were checking up on you, and you were not depressed at all?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it was at least a few weeks. It was before the depression really took hold. I was still running the little classes at the shop, showing people how to set stones and all that, and I’d go home and mess around in the garden—tomatoes, mostly, though they never come in evenly.\n\nBut even then, when I wasn’t really depressed, I kept hearing something from the polishing room, like my name or a noise that made me check. And I had this worry that customers were watching the business too closely, like checking up on us. So… yes, that did happen when I wasn’t feeling that heavy sadness yet.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences start after any major medical illness, head injury, or seizure?\n\nPatient: I don’t… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I can point to. No big accident or seizure that I know of.\n\nI did once bump my head on the edge of a display case years ago—those glass corners are awful, and I told my assistant we needed better padding—but it wasn’t anything major. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself becoming less expressive emotionally, like your face showing less feeling or your voice sounding flatter than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed my face doing anything different, exactly. My sister once said I looked “tired,” but she says that when I don’t wear lipstick too, so I don’t know if that counts.\n\nMy voice… I don’t think it’s flatter. At the shop I still talk to people about stones and settings, and sometimes I probably sound too interested, honestly, if someone asks about opals. So… no, I haven’t really noticed that, but maybe other people would know better than me.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems with hearing your name, feeling people were checking up on you, and your speech getting hard to follow been going on for 6 months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… longer than six months. It’s been, I guess, almost two years now, since not long after we took on the business mortgage.\n\nThat whole period is a little tangled in my head, because the mortgage paperwork was such a mess, and I remember thinking the bank forms were printed in the tiniest font… but yes, the hearing my name and feeling like people were checking up on me, and the talking getting hard to follow sometimes—those changes have been going on since around then. Almost two years.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech is often hard to follow or doesn't make sense?\n\nPatient: Yes… people have said that. Mostly customers, and my assistant too, in a nicer way.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, actually. Someone will ask me something simple, like when a ring repair will be ready, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the invoice, and then somehow garden soil—because I had to repot the basil by the back window—and then the mortgage papers from the business, and I can see their face change like they’re trying to follow me but they can’t.\n\nI don’t always notice it right away, but I can tell afterward that I lost the point somewhere.\n\nDoctor: When your speech becomes hard for people to follow, is that usually happening even when you are not hearing your name or feeling that people are checking up on you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, it can happen even when I’m not hearing anything like that or feeling checked on. That’s actually why I came in, because it’ll be something ordinary—someone asks when a ring repair will be ready—and I mean to answer plainly.\n\nBut then I’m talking about the invoice, and then I’ll jump to the garden soil, because I was thinking about repotting something by the window, and then somehow I’m back on the mortgage papers… and I can see their eyes sort of go blank, like I’ve taken them somewhere they didn’t ask to go. So yes, it’s not only when those other things are happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you been behaving in ways that other people find very disorganized or odd?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “odd” is hard for me to judge because people think jewelry people are odd anyway. I’ll get very particular about a setting or a stone tray being just so, and then someone says I’m acting strange, but I think I’m just trying not to lose a tiny sapphire, you know?\n\nI don’t think I’ve been doing anything very wild or… disorganized like that, not that I can point to. Maybe I’d have to ask what you mean exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your behavior seems very disorganized or bizarre?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has said it that plainly, like “bizarre.” My assistant has said I seem scattered sometimes, but she says that when I’ve moved three trays and then can’t remember which one has the clasp I was working on… and honestly that happens in a jewelry shop, things are small, the light catches them—\n\nSorry. I don’t know. I haven’t had people tell me my behavior is very disorganized or bizarre, not in those words. Maybe they notice things I don’t, but I can’t point to anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost motivation to do everyday things, like work, chores, or hobbies, even when you want to do them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. I still want to do things, at least in my head. I used to get really excited hunting for old clasps, the unusual ones with the little hinge that still moves smoothly, or planning small jewelry classes—like showing people how to tell when a setting is too worn.\n\nBut lately I mostly just stand there and stare at the trays. I’ll think, “I should start sorting these,” or “I should sketch out that class,” and then it all feels… not worth starting. Like the first step is too far away. And then I end up just moving one tray from one side of the bench to the other, which isn’t really doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your emotional reactions seem odd or inappropriate for the situation, like smiling or laughing when something isn’t funny?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that anyone’s told me. I mean, sometimes I smile when I’m nervous, which people can take wrong, but I don’t remember anyone saying I was laughing at the wrong time or anything like that.\n\nIn the shop, people get very serious over sentimental pieces, like a grandmother’s ring, and I try to be careful with my face because jewelry carries all these stories… but no, I can’t really think of someone telling me my reactions seemed odd or inappropriate.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that you seem emotionally flat or less expressive than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure anyone has said that to me, no. Not “flat.” My daughter once said I was hard to read, but that was during one of those calls where the connection kept cutting out, and honestly everyone looks odd on video, frozen with their mouth half open.\n\nAt the shop, I do try to keep my face calm with customers, because if someone brings in a piece that’s cracked or the stone is loose, you don’t want to gasp and make them panic. So maybe I seem less expressive there, but I can’t think of anyone telling me I’m emotionally flat or different than I used to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 19\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
{"key":"None","name":"None","description":"None","reason":"The question is about diminished emotional expression or flat affect, but the provided symptom profile does not include an item covering flat or reduced facial emotional expression."}

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you lost motivation to do everyday things, like work, chores, or hobbies, even when you want to do them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. I still want to do things, at least in my head. I used to get really excited hunting for old clasps, the unusual ones with the little hinge that still moves smoothly, or planning small jewelry classes—like showing people how to tell when a setting is too worn.\n\nBut lately I mostly just stand there and stare at the trays. I’ll think, “I should start sorting these,” or “I should sketch out that class,” and then it all feels… not worth starting. Like the first step is too far away. And then I end up just moving one tray from one side of the bench to the other, which isn’t really doing anything.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your emotional reactions seem odd or inappropriate for the situation, like smiling or laughing when something isn’t funny?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that anyone’s told me. I mean, sometimes I smile when I’m nervous, which people can take wrong, but I don’t remember anyone saying I was laughing at the wrong time or anything like that.\n\nIn the shop, people get very serious over sentimental pieces, like a grandmother’s ring, and I try to be careful with my face because jewelry carries all these stories… but no, I can’t really think of someone telling me my reactions seemed odd or inappropriate.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that you seem emotionally flat or less expressive than you used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure anyone has said that to me, no. Not “flat.” My daughter once said I was hard to read, but that was during one of those calls where the connection kept cutting out, and honestly everyone looks odd on video, frozen with their mouth half open.\n\nAt the shop, I do try to keep my face calm with customers, because if someone brings in a piece that’s cracked or the stone is loose, you don’t want to gasp and make them panic. So maybe I seem less expressive there, but I can’t think of anyone telling me I’m emotionally flat or different than I used to be.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave people often told you that your face shows very little emotion, even in situations where most people would look clearly happy, sad, or upset?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 53 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that people have said that to me, no. I mean, I can get quiet, and when I’m concentrating on a tiny stone or trying not to bend a prong, my face probably does go blank—my old teacher used to say jewelers all get that “bench face,” like you’re staring through the metal.

But in situations like happy or sad… I’m not sure. Nobody’s come out and told me my face shows very little emotion. At least not that I remember.
